Beilinson-Parshin adeles via solid algebraic geometry
Christopher Brav and Grigorii Konovalov

Abstract
. In this paper, we apply Clausen-Scholze’s theory of solid modules to the existence of adelic decompositions for schemes of finite type over ℤ \mathbb{Z} . Specifically, we use the six-functor formalism for solid modules to define the skeletal filtration of a scheme, and then we show that decomposing a quasi-coherent sheaf with respect to this filtration gives rise to a new construction of the Beilinson-Parshin adelic resolution. As an application of the adelic decomposition combined with some nice completeness properties of the solid tensor product, we prove a version of adelic descent for solid quasi-coherent sheaves.



Introduction

Recall the ‘Hasse fracture square’ or ‘adelic decomposition’, which reconstructs the integers from its
p
-completions and its rationalization. Namely, in the derived category of abelian groups there is a homotopy pullback square

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\otimes\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}

More generally, there is an analogous square for any object of the derived category of abelian groups. Parshin
[
1
]
, then Beilinson
[
2
]
, constructed a higher dimensional generalization of the adelic decomposition that gives a functorial resolution of a quasi-coherent sheaf on a nice scheme in terms of iterated localisations and completions along all flags of closed integral subschemes.

The goal of this paper is to show how Clausen-Scholze’s condensed mathematics
[
3
]
, more specifically the theory of solid modules, leads to a new construction of Beilinson-Parshin adelic decompositions for schemes of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}
.
1
1

1

For discussing of more general cases, see comment
0.1.3
at the end of the introduction.
The basic idea is to use the enhanced locality and functoriality of the category of solid modules to define the skeletal filtration of a scheme, then to decompose a solid quasi-coherent sheaf (in particular, a usual quasi-coherent sheaf) with respect to this filtration. The result is a reconstruction of any (solid) quasi-coherent sheaf via a homotopy limit cube, generalizing the Hasse square above. For a perfect complex, the vertices are given as products over flags of integral closed subschemes of iterated completed localizations, just as in the Beilinson-Parshin resolution, and the general case is reduced to this via commutation with colimits.

By contrast to the constructions of Parshin
[
1
]
, Beilinson
[
2
]
, and Balchin-Greenlees
[
4
]
, which put iterated completed localizations into the construction of the resolution and check exactness of the resulting complex, in our construction the iterated completed localizations arise a posteriori, as the output of the decomposition of a quasi-coherent sheaf with respect to the skeletal filtration.

In order to motivate the construction, consider a nice topological space
X
together with a closed subspace
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
and its open complement
j:U\hookrightarrow X
. Then the six-functor formalism for the (derived category) of abelian sheaves
Sh(X)
gives a recollement

\textstyle{Sh(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{i_{*}}}
\textstyle{Sh(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\>!}}
\scriptstyle{i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}}
\textstyle{Sh(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\;.}
\scriptstyle{j_{*}}
\scriptstyle{{j}_{!}}

From this we obtain for any complex of abelian sheaves
F
two recollement triangles from restriction to
Z
and to
U
:

j_{!}j^{*}F\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow i_{*}i^{*}F

i_{*}i^{!}F\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow j_{*}j^{*}F

We note however that in general, the functor
j_{*}
does not commute with all direct sums, so the second triangle can be difficult to compute.)

Comparing the two types of restrictions, we obtain a homotopy pullback square

\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j^{*}j_{*}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{*}i^{*}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}j^{*}F}

Consider now a scheme
X
of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}
and its (derived) category of quasi-coherent sheaves
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
. Given a closed subspace
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
, and its open complement
j:U\hookrightarrow X
, we have continuous adjunctions
j^{*}:\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\leftrightarrow\operatorname{QCoh}(U):j_{*}
and
i_{*}:\operatorname{QCoh}_{Z}(X)\leftrightarrow\operatorname{QCoh}(X):i^{!}
, where
\operatorname{QCoh}_{Z}(X)
is the full subcategory of quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
‘supported’ in
Z
. However, the left adjoint
j_{!}
of
j_{*}
usually does not exist, since
j^{*}
does not preserve arbitrary products. Similarly, the left adjoint
i^{*}
of
i_{*}
usually does not exist. We therefore have only one half of a recollement, namely the triangle

i_{*}i^{!}F\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow j_{*}j^{*}F.

In short, even for some of the simplest maps of schemes, there is not a full six-functor formalism for quasi-coherent sheaves.

However, using condensed mathematics, Clausen and Scholze (
[
3
]
,
[
5
]
,
[
6
]
) have shown how to enlarge the context of algebraic geometry to an ‘analytic geometry’ built on analytic spectra
\operatorname{Spec}^{an}(A)
of ‘analytic rings’
A
and more general spaces glued from such spectra. Quasi-coherent sheaves on analytic spaces then admit a full six-functor formalism for a much wider class of maps. In particular, there is a fully faithful embedding of usual algebraic geometry into analytic geometry that takes the usual spectrum
\operatorname{Spec}(A)
of a discrete ring
A
to the analytic spectrum
\operatorname{Spec}^{an}(A_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}})
of the ‘solid analytic ring structure’
A_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}
on
A
. Concretely, this means specifying a certain category
A_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}-\mathrm{Mod}_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}
of ‘complete’
A
-modules generated under colimits by products
\prod_{I}A
over index sets
I
. By definition, the quasi-coherent sheaves
\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{Spec}^{an}(A_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}))
on
\operatorname{Spec}^{an}(A_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}})
is the category
A_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}-\mathrm{Mod}_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}
of ‘complete’ or ‘solid’
A
-modules, which admits a natural continuous and symmetric monoidal embedding
A-\mathrm{Mod}\hookrightarrow A_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}-\mathrm{Mod}_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}
. We emphasize however that the embedding does not in general preserve infinite limits. Indeed, the whole point of the category
A_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}-\mathrm{Mod}_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}
is to ensure that infinite limits are somehow appropriately complete.

Now consider
X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
,
Z=\operatorname{Spec}(A/I)\subset X
a closed subscheme, and
U=X\setminus Z
the open complement, and consider the embedding of these objects
X_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}},U_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}},Z_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}
into analytic geometry. The first new phenomenon that one encounters in applying the six-functor formalism to solid quasi-coherent sheaves is that the kernel of the restriction functor
j^{*}:\operatorname{QCoh}(X_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}})\rightarrow\operatorname{QCoh}(U_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}})
consists not just of solid quasi-coherent sheaves supported on the complement
Z_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}
, but rather on its tubular neighborhood
\widetilde{Z}_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}=\operatorname{Spec}^{an}(A^{\wedge}_{I})
. Here,
A^{\wedge}_{I}
denotes the completion of the ring
A
at the ideal
I
, computed as a limit inside solid modules, together with its induced analytic ring structure over
A_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}
.

Altogether, we obtain a recollement

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 29.22783pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-29.22783pt\raise 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.63542pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{j}_{!}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 145.19736pt\raise 8.61108pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

and therefore, as in the topological case, a homotopy pullback square reconstructing any quasi-coherent sheaf
F
on
X_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}
from its restrictions to the tubular neighborhood and its complement. Moreover, this recollement is continuous in the sense that all functors preserve (homotopy) colimits, thus solid quasi-coherent sheaves simulataneously enjoy the best properties both of sheaves on nice topological spaces and quasi-coherent sheaves on nice schemes.

The key idea of this paper is that the Beilinson-Parshin adelic decomposition of a quasi-coherent sheaf on a scheme
X
of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}
and of dimension
d
arises by considering a sequence of recollements associated to a skeletal filtration

{Z}_{0}\subset{Z}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset{Z}_{d-1}\subset X_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}

where
{Z}_{k}
is in a certain sense the union of all tubular neighbourhoods of closed subschemes of dimension at most
k
. Formally, we define

Z_{k}=\operatorname{Spec}^{an}(A^{\wedge}_{k})
, where
A^{\wedge}_{k}
is as a solid ring is defined as the limit of all completions at all ideals of dimension at most
k
.

Given any filtration
Z_{0}\subset Z_{1}\subset\dots\subset Z_{d-1}\subset Z_{d}=X_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}
, define strata
X_{k}=Z_{k}\setminus Z_{k-1}
with inclusions
s_{k}:X_{k}\hookrightarrow X
. For each stratum we have a localization functor
L_{k}:={s_{k}}_{*}s^{*}_{k}:\operatorname{QCoh}(X_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{QCoh}(X_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}})
equipped with a unit of adjunction
\id\longrightarrow{s_{k}}_{*}s^{*}_{k}=L_{k}
. For a tuple
d\geq k_{m}>\dots>k_{0}\geq 0
, we can form the iterated localisation functor
L_{k_{0}}\circ\dots\circ L_{k_{m}}
, and using the units of adjunction, the iterated localisations fit together into a commutative ‘fracture cube’ of functors. For example, in the case
d=2
, the cube is of the form

\textstyle{L_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\id\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{0}L_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{0}L_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{0}L_{1}L_{2}}
\textstyle{L_{1}L_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
.

In any dimension, using the theory of total homotopy fibres
[
7
]
[
4
]
, or of non-commutative stratifications
[
8
]
, the corresponding punctured cube exhibits a sheaf
F
as the homotopy limit of the rest cube.

The main idea of this paper is to analyse the skeletal filtration
Z_{0}\subset Z_{1}\dots Z_{d-1}\subset Z_{d}=X_{{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}
of a
d
-dimensional finite type scheme and to show that the corresponding fracture cube for a solid quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
gives a Beilinson-Parshin adelic decomposition expressed in terms of filtered colimits of iterated completed localizations over flags of integral closed subschemes. More precisely, our main theorem is Theorem
2.3.1
, a special case of which we state here for concreteness:

Theorem 0.0.1
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring of finite type over
\mathbf{Z}
. Then the vertices of the fracture cube applied to
A
are given as products over nested chains of prime ideals over completed localisations, as in the usual Beilinson-Parshin formula:

L_{k_{0}}L_{k_{1}}\ldots L_{k_{m}}A\;\simeq\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{p}_{m}\\
\mathrm{dim}\mathfrak{p}_{m}=k_{m}\end{subarray}}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\text{\phantom{a}}}\limits}}\!\!}\>(\ldots\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{p}_{1}\supset\mathfrak{p}_{2}\\
\mathrm{dim}\mathfrak{p}_{1}=k_{1}\end{subarray}}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\text{\phantom{a}}}\limits}}\!\!}\>(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{p}_{0}\supset\mathfrak{p}_{1}\\
\mathrm{dim}\mathfrak{p}_{0}=k_{0}\end{subarray}}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\text{\phantom{a}}}\limits}}\!\!}\>A_{\mathfrak{p}_{0}})_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}\ldots)_{\mathfrak{p}_{m}}\;.

Moreover, the fracture cube for a general solid module commutes with colimits, products, and completions.

Let us emphasise again that our cubical decomposition works for a general solid quasi-coherent sheaf and is constructed from the geometry of the skeletal filtration, with the flags of closed integral subschemes and the completed localizations appearing a posteriori. The advantage of this point of view is that it suggests various generalizations, by decomposing solid quasi-coherent sheaves with respect to other naturally arising filtrations of schemes, for example the tangent rank stratification for a morphism or the orbit-closure stratification for a scheme with group action. In all of these cases, it would be interesting to study the corresponding glued t-structures. We intend to pursue these ideas in future work.

As an application of the adelic decomposition we prove a version of adelic descent (cf.
[
9
]
) in the solid setting.
Here we give a brief description of the descent statement and also of some relevant technical results which may be of independent interest.
As in
0.0.1
,
A
denotes a dimension
d
finite type commutative ring over
\mathbb{Z}
. The main theorem implies that
A
can be decomposed as
a cubical limit:

A\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\lim}\limits}}L_{k_{0}}\ldots L_{k_{m}}A\;.

In particular, there is a fully faithful functor

\mathrm{Mod}_{A_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}\;\longrightarrow\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\lim}\limits}}L_{k_{0}}\ldots L_{k_{m}}A-\mathrm{Mod}_{A_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}\;.

The affine case of the solid adelic descent statement (Theorem
4.2.4
) proved in this paper states that this functor is an equivalence.
We note that, although the fully faithfulness follows immediately from the adelic decomposition applied to
A
, the essential surjectivity
requires further work and in particular uses certain nice completeness properties of the solid tensor product, see
Proposition
4.1.4
.

To be specific, we prove (see
4.2.1
and
4.2.2
)
that each adelic ring of higher length splits as a tensor product:

L_{k_{0}}\ldots L_{k_{m}}A-\mathrm{Mod}_{A_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}\;\simeq\;L_{k_{0}}A\otimes_{A_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}\ldots\otimes_{A_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}L_{k_{m}}A\;;

and that each each length
1
adelic ring is idempotent over
A_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}
. This in particular implies that each adelic ring is idempotent over
A_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}
,
from which the essential surjectivity follows right away.

There is also a global version of the solid adelic descent, which holds for a scheme
X
of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}

and can be summarized as follows: the canonical map

\spec_{X_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}(L_{0}\mathcal{O}_{X})\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\spec_{X_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}(L_{\mathrm{dim}(X)}\mathcal{O}_{X})\;\longrightarrow\;X_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}

is a !-closed cover (see Lecture 18 of
[
6
]
for the definition),
and the intersections are given by the relative spectra of the adelic rings of higher length.
We also prove (Proposition
4.3.1
) that each term
\spec_{X_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}(L_{k}\mathcal{O}_{X})
and any multiple intersection of such
are in fact affinoid (i.e. spectra of analytic rings).

The paper is structured as follows. In the first section, we review some general solid algebra and construct the recollement of solid modules associated to the tubular neighborhood of a closed subscheme and its complement. In the second section, we construct and analyse the skeletal filtration of a finite type affine scheme and prove the affine case of our main theorem. In the third section, we explain how to globalise our construction to non-affine schemes. In the final section, we state and prove a version of adelic descent in the solid setting.

0.1.
Notation and conventions

0.1.1.

In this paper, we work in the setting of
\infty
-categories. We adopt the usual convention that the word category means

(\infty,1)
-category, and all the standard categorical notions and constructions, such as functors and (co)limits, should be
understood in this context. In particular, we shall typically refer to derived completion simply as completion.

0.1.2.

We will denote by
\stab
the
(\infty,1)
-category of stable presentable categories.

0.1.3.

Throughout the paper, we work with commutative, unital rings
A
of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}
.
We chose to work over
\mathbb{Z}
for convenience, but it’s possible to construct the skeletal filtration and prove the main theorem for a
finite type ring over a field—one should define
the base category of ‘solid’
k
-modules synthetically, mimicking the synthetic description of the category of solid abelian
groups given in Corollary 6.1 of
[
3
]
, and then all the relevant arguments of
[
3
]
and this paper
should apply without change.

Moreover, the dot analytic ring structure on a localisation, which is defined in
1.4
,
makes it possible to work with a ring essentially of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}
or a field.

For the case of a general Noetherian scheme, the natural approach within the world of solid algebraic geometry seems to be to work with filtered colimits of completions to define tubular neighborhoods. However, this would seem to produce a variant of Beilinson-Parshin adeles, in which the local factors are expressed not as completed localizations of general Noetherian rings, but as filtered colimits of completed localizations of finite type subrings.

0.1.4.

Let
Z\subseteq\spec(A)
be a closed subset defined by an ideal
I\subset A
. We will use both
A^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}_{I}
and
A^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}_{Z}

to denote the (derived) completion of
A
at the ideal
I
. The same goes for the completion of a (solid) module over
A
.

For a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}\subset A
, we will denote by
{\mathrel{{\mathop{\text{\phantom{a}}}\limits}}\!\!}A_{\mathfrak{p}}
the local ring
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
completed at the maximal ideal.
Similarly, for any (solid)
A
-module
M
,
{\mathrel{{\mathop{\text{\phantom{a}}}\limits}}\!\!}\>M_{\mathfrak{p}}
denotes the localization of
M
at
\mathfrak{p}
followed by the (derived) completion
at the maximal ideal of
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

0.1.5.

We use Lurie’s notation for categories of modules over an algebra as opposed to the traditional derived category notation. For example,

\mathrm{Mod}_{A}
means the whole stable
(\infty,1)
-category of modules over the algebra
A
. Likewise,
\mathrm{Mod}_{A_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}}}
denotes
the stable
(\infty,1)
-category of solid
A
-modules, and we would like to bring it to the reader’s attention that
Clausen and Scholze adopt different conventions in
[
3
]
.

This also applies to categories of quasi-coherent sheaves over a scheme:
\text{QCoh}(X)
means the stable
(\infty,1)
-category of
quasi-coherent complexes over
X
;
\text{QCoh}(X_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare$}}}}})
means the stable
(\infty,1)
-category of solid quasi-coherent sheaves over
X
.

We would like to thank Sergei Gorchinsky, Dmitry Kaledin, Mikhail Kapranov, Nick Rozenblyum, Denis Osipov, and Artem Prikhodko for conversations about
adeles and for their interest in this project.
